By my cultivating merit,
I have set my mind on supreme awakening.
The Teacher abides in supreme bliss,
And by that he works for the benefit of sentient beings.
By proclaiming the sound of the syllable hūṃ,
The proud obstructors grow afraid.
Through your close attendance
They have no birth or death.
They play as the King of Bliss itself,
They journey into the mind of awakening,
They become blended together with you,
And abide as one single mass, nondual."
When they had praised him thus, the Great Vajra
Taught the four offerings.
Being nondual with the lord of the maṇḍala,
One makes the offerings nondual.
Substances whose nature is outer and inner
Are held to be the second offering.
Delighting in food, drink and the like
Is the third, the nondual offering.
The fourth is the offering
Of upholding the Dharma and yoga.

Relying on such offerings,
By doing all of them one becomes
A king of actions. Vajraḍāka
Should be pleased in the triangle.
First having made the maṇḍala,
With the left hand, in every case,
The vessel, circled with the lotus,
Should be held by the mantrins.
First, to the yoginīs,
With hair unbound, give the torma.
All the yoginīs should take the water-born
By its mouth and stir it.
The glory that has come into that one's hand
No one whatsoever can steal away.
Just as bliss is grasped through bliss,
Please the supreme perfect Buddha.
Always keep the pledges,
And follow the conduct of the vajra siblings as well.
Come under the sway of supreme compassion,
And meditate on the senses as impermanent;
Abide having mastered
The nature that is without appearance.
Through the union of vajra and lotus,
The mudrā is the supreme inner self.
The yogin of nondual emptiness
Should abandon all entities.
Here one should practice that very ultimate;
Always recollect the mind of awakening.
By meditating on a single instruction
One should realize all the instructions.
Just as by attaining a single ground
One comes to attain all the grounds,
So too all the instructions
Are attained through a single instruction.
Toward the young and the old
And toward the yoginīs, in every case,
The wise should act
With the rite of equal offering.
Through inequality accomplishment is destroyed,
And one will fall into the hells and so on.
The elders are the abode of all qualities;
Pleasing them bestows awakening.
As a special offering here,
For the young and likewise,
Yogins together with their partners
Should do the circling of flowers and so on.
Never speak words of reproach;
Be free of engaging with the wanted and the unwanted.
Take even what is not given,
Always kill living beings,
Speak lies to sentient beings,
And go to the yoginīs, the mother and the rest.
Continually perform the circle worship,
And give the five foods and so on.
What is gained through that merit
Is freedom from obstructors at all times,
And, through seeing accomplished yoginīs,
A different state of mind fully arises.
From the union of all the heroes,
Vajrasattva, supreme in bliss,
Vajraḍāka, the Tathāgata,
The Blessed Lord, spoke this.

This is the fifty-first chapter, called "Teaching the Nondual Pledge: the Praise, the Four Offerings and So On," from the Great King of Tantras, the Glorious Ḍākārṇava Yoginī Tantra.

This tantra that has come forth:
What never arose before will not arise again.
It is complete with all characteristics,
And free of all that lacks them.
The yoga embodying the thirty-seven,
The nondual Ḍākārṇava,
The nondual root tantra, the great tantra,
Has the form of all the aspects assembled.
At all times it abides
In the mouths of the buddhas and bodhisattvas.
It is the casket of all the letters.
First, in three million six hundred thousand,
The Ḍākārṇava was expounded;
The great one was then spoken as a short one.
It was spoken by all the ḍākas,
As many as the atoms of Mount Meru.
Infinite and boundless,
Within the earth in the age of strife,
The infinite Blessed One spoke this.
The tantra spoken by Śākyasiṃha
Has gone to the far shore of yoga.
The listener is the Teacher himself,
And the expounders are other persons too.
What has been well spoken and what will be expounded
Will also proceed into its very nature.
The compilers and the others
Obtained what proceeds from me.
All the mātṛkās, assembled,
Compiled it in the vajra sites.
What each wished of the other
They requested, making supplication.
In these words
Spoken by the Blessed One they rejoiced.
Go to liberation from evils;
Go forth from existence!
From the union of all the heroes,
Vajrasattva, supreme in bliss,
Vajraḍāka, the Tathāgata,
The Blessed Lord, spoke this.

Here ends the great king of tantras called "Yoga, Wisdom, Saṃvara, Action and Suchness Become an Ocean in the Six Oceans and So On, Unequaled by These, from the Ḍākārṇava," from the Great King of Tantras, the Glorious Ḍākārṇava Yoginī Tantra.

At the great supreme site of accomplishment in glorious Nepal, in the great temple Yutung Lhündrup, it was heard and translated from the lips of the great Indian paṇḍita and vajra master Jayasena, son of the Śākyas, crown jewel of logicians and unrivaled in the present day, whose heart is moistened with great compassion, who satisfies beings gathered from many directions with a rain of Dharma and material gifts, and who touches the pollen of the lotus feet of glorious Vajravārāhī, together with the Tibetan translator, the Śākya monk Dharma Yönten. Later, a person possessing the eye of Dharma revised it well, in conformity also with the manner of the vajra word of the Glorious Paramādya.

The bodies of the buddhas, equal to space, blissful, perfect, and emanated in various ways,
Speak what is stainless; by the force of the various dispositions of those to be trained,
Even a single utterance teaches many and various meanings, so it is hard to examine.
Even so, when a lord of men, mounted on a great chariot,
Enters the battlefront, do not the foot soldiers strive to fight?
Likewise, following the great scholars of the past,
Though I am not capable of benefiting others, I have translated with the wish to help.
Wherever here, through the fault not of the text or the guru but of my own understanding,
Word and meaning have come into conflict, may the Blessed One, the heroes and yoginīs,
The hosts of ḍākinīs, the gods who delight in the teaching, and the learned be patient.
By whatever small stainless virtue is gained from this,
May all beings attain the state of Vajradhara.
In this, which I have merely pioneered,
When those who know how word and meaning apply,
Speakers of both languages, look at it,
I ask them to correct whatever appears to be faulty.
Do not deride it out of jealousy,
But please rejoice in it as well.

It is complete.

Translator's notes

The opening speech is introduced as spoken by the Lord of Great Heroes, but from its second half (with the humble verb "I shall listen" and "I, Vārāhī, ask") the speaker is evidently Vārāhī; the translation follows the Tibetan as it stands. "Dānavas" renders sbyin byed, whose identification is not certain. "The three planes of existence" renders sa gsum, the three levels (below, upon, and above the earth). The line on the world ages (rdzogs ldan gsum gnyis rtsod dus) is read as Kṛta, Tretā ("the third"), Dvāpara ("the second"), and Kali. Most of the yoginīs' names are rendered by meaning, since their Sanskrit forms cannot be securely identified; only Padminī, Śaṅkhinī, Citriṇī, and Hastinī, the four classical types of women, are given in Sanskrit. The list as written yields more than thirty-seven items, so some adjacent words may form single names. "She of Taste" renders ro ma, which in this sense-object series is a name rather than the rasanā channel. "Hara and Gaurī" renders 'phrog byed dkar mo, read as the standard Tibetan forms of these two names; it could also be a single figure, "the white Haraṇī." "The Vaibhāṣikas who hold to the modes of conduct" renders spyod lam la sogs bye brag chos, read as a reference to the Vaibhāṣika school. This section encodes seed syllables by describing letters by their class in the alphabet and the vowels and marks added to them. The coded references are translated literally and not decoded. "The letter that ends with sa" (sa mtha') is read as the letter following sa, that is, ha. "The Terrifying Lady" renders yongs su skrag byed ma, whose Sanskrit form is uncertain. As in the preceding section, the seed syllables are encoded by describing letters, and these descriptions are translated literally without decoding. "Vasu, arrow, and moon" and "vasu and veda" are read as standard number words (vasu for eight, arrow for five, moon for one, veda for four), so they probably encode numbers; they are kept as words. "Those who speak crookedly" renders yon por smra ba, whose exact sense is unclear. The seed syllables are again encoded by describing letters, and these descriptions are translated literally. "The third of the sibilants" renders dro ba'i gsum pa, taking dro ba ("warm") as the Sanskrit grammatical class of sibilants (ūṣman). A few lines remain obscure as written, notably "All the forests of pha wane" and "Death in the seal is conception." The passage encodes mantra syllables by their place in the Tibetan-Sanskrit syllabary (for example "the last of the pa class" for ma); the encodings are rendered literally. The line read as "Nga is appearance without an object" is uncertain: nga may be the syllable ṅa or the first-person pronoun "I". "Lord of voice" renders dbyangs kyi bdag po, apparently the tongue. Bola and kakkola are the tantric code terms for the male and female organs. The ten wrathful ones are rendered as Yamāntaka and the others, taking gshin rje and mthar byed, split across two verse lines, as the single name. "Ḍākinīsaṃvara" and "Abhidhāna" render mkha' 'gro sdom pa and mngon brjod, taken as titles of related Saṃvara scriptures. The line rendered "What has not arisen will not arise" (ma byung 'byung bar mi 'gyur) is obscure in context. The list of the eastern land (dus sbyor, ro hi ta, chu srin, rgyal, nyi ma, dga' skyed) appears to be a coded set of correspondences (time, the rohita fish, the makara, and so on) whose exact reference is unclear; it is rendered literally. "Crusher of Thunderbolts" renders thog 'joms pa, apparently the name of the last goddess of the series, not securely identified. "The Beautiful One" (mdzes ma) is taken as the speaker's self-designation, presumably Vārāhī. "The Ram, the Tawny One and the Good-Minded" (lug, dmar ser, yid bzang) seem to be coded calendrical or astrological terms (cf. the sign Meṣa) and are rendered literally. "Taught as the surplus qualities" (yon tan 'phar ma) is obscure. Slob dpon sems kyi rdo rje is taken as the master Cittavajra; it could also be read as "the master, the vajra of mind." The verses headed by the syllables ā, rṇṇa, va, ma, hā, sa, mu, dra spell out the Sanskrit ārṇava mahāsamudra ("ocean, great sea") as an acrostic. "Chudün" (chu dun) is obscure; it may be a place in Jambudvīpa or a corrupt term for the present age, and is left as written. "The day of the lord of the earth" (sa yi dbang phyug zhag) is taken as a weekday name, and mchu as the lunar mansion Maghā. Many of the place names in the list of sixty-four goddesses are transliterations whose Sanskrit forms are uncertain (for example Tirahudati, Daddaranaṭa, Ḍikakari, Jajjayuta, Triśakta, Cambha, Luhara, Purahorika, Mummunam, Bhaṭṭo, Siddhaśrimbali); they are given as the Tibetan spells them. The syllable la before "gri ha de ba" has been taken as part of the transliteration of Gṛhadevatā. The heart-wheel list is said to contain eight but nine items appear as the text stands; it is rendered as written. "Great Glorious One" (dpal chen mo) is translated literally, its intended place or goddess being unidentified. "Field-born ones" renders the obscure ང་ཞིང་མ, read as zhing ma (kṣetrajā); the sense is uncertain. Among the thirty-two head goddesses, those not confidently identifiable with a Sanskrit name (She Who Bares Her Fangs, the Terrifying One, She Who Has Passed Beyond, She of Fierce Form, Stout Kālī, the Victress, the Hostile One, She of the Moon, the Fierce-Eyed One, the Outcaste Woman, the Princess, She of Great Magic) are translated by meaning. The "two faculties" may refer, in tantric usage, to the two sexual organs. The syllable pral in the first line is rendered as "dissolution" (reading pralaya); this is conjectural. The second couplet (the rising of the Fish, Desire, the lunar mansion, Supreme Throat) is obscure as astronomical terminology and is translated literally. "Goḍi" in the comparison about letters is unidentified and left as spelled. The line about Aries, the Bird and Kula is uncertain; Kula is left as spelled. The mantra syllables set in the four wheels appear to be the initial syllables of the names of the goddesses listed in the previous chapter. "Machine" renders 'khrul 'khor (yantra), here a yogic device of the channels and winds. "Skull Fragment" (thod pa'i dum bu) is the name of a hero not confidently identified with a Sanskrit form, so it is translated literally. In the list of deities, "Passion" ('dod chags) is translated literally, its referent (probably the buddha of the passion family) being unstated; bzang po is taken as Samantabhadra, and 'dod among the wrathful kings as Ṭakkirāja. "The causes" (rgyu) before Vairocana and the rest may be an error for rgyud, "lines" or "tantras". The syllables kā, kā and syā in the seventh chapter spell out the name Kākāsyā. The five secondary winds are named in the Tibetan as nāga, tortoise, lizard, Devadatta, and Dhanaṃjaya (Skt. nāga, kūrma, kṛkara, devadatta, dhanaṃjaya). The four goddesses brtan ma, pho nya, mche ma and 'joms ma are taken as Yamadāḍhī, Yamadūtī, Yamadaṃṣṭrī and Yamamathanī, the four corner goddesses of the Saṃvara maṇḍala, though the Tibetan omits the element "Yama". "Right action" renders yang dag las mtha', the factor of the noble eightfold path (samyakkarmānta). The syllables u, lū, ka and u, la, ū, kā, sya spell out the name Ulūkāsyā. In the list of the six yogas, ཕག་མོ is taken here as Śūkarāsyā, the sow-faced gate goddess of this group (with the crow-, owl- and dog-faced ones), rather than Vārāhī. "Plume" renders the obscure sgro ba in the list of bodily sites; its intended location is uncertain. Bolla and kakkola are coded tantric terms for the male and female sexual organs. The syllables śvā, na and āsya spell out the name Śvānāsyā. The list of "supreme medicines" (Padminī, Śaṅkhinī, Citriṇī, Hastinī and the rest) appears to name classes of women or consorts; several items (rendered "the pale-nosed one," "the one adorned with ointment," "Sun-born," "Snake-eye") could not be identified with confidence and are translated by meaning. The Tibetan chu stong is read as chu stod, the month Āṣāḍha, parallel to the other month and zodiac names (Karkaṭa, Siṃha, Śrāvaṇa, Makara). "Near field" reads nyid zhing as nye zhing (upakṣetra). The crow and owl in the account of gestation evidently allude to the goddesses Kākāsyā and Ulūkāsyā. Several items in the lists of animal faces and hand implements are uncertain in identification (for example muñja, the "heron," "marrow" for gnye ma, and bhokaṃḍaka, left as given); they are rendered by their most likely sense. "Mohita" (bewilderment) is taken as a ritual function associated with the goddess. The calendrical verses on the wheel for ransoming death use number symbols (fire, moon, directions, arrows, earth, wealth, waters and so on) whose exact values in this system are uncertain; they are translated literally. The letters ya, ma, dā, a and ḍhi in the first section spell out the name Yamadāḍhī. The letters ya, ma, daṃ, ṭa, ṣa, i, ṣṭi and nī given in the meditation spell out the goddess's name, Yamadaṃṣṭrī (and in the first section ya and ma begin the name of Yamadūtī). "Śakra has become balanced" and "Rudra too has become balanced" are literal; these names appear to stand for particular winds or maṇḍalas of the body, and the exact sense is uncertain. "Honor should be shown to the heroes" reads rjed as an honoring verb; as written it could mean "forget." "The ten roots" is probably the medicinal compound daśamūla. The line rendered "Again the goddess was told" (slar yang lha mo des gsungs pa) could also be read "Again the goddess said"; what follows reads as continued instruction, so it is taken as addressed to her. "Those with such merit" translates bsod nams ldan rnams literally; the sense is evidently that buddha wisdom lies beyond those of the meager merit of the Kali age. The verses on the syllables śrī, he, ru, and ka give a syllable-by-syllable explanation of the name Heruka; several lines are terse and their exact construal is uncertain. The deity pressed down with Gaurī, rendered Hara, is literally "the Ravisher," taken here as an epithet of Śiva. Several items in the final list of attributes are uncertain: "lynx" renders dbyi gu, "chain" renders gdos (a word of unclear meaning), and "dubhusa" is a transliterated term left as it stands; "fishing net" may also be read as part of the preceding item. Several names in the six-by-six lists of yoginīs are not securely identifiable and have been translated by meaning (She Who Nourishes, She Who Transforms Others, She With a Child, She Who Follows, the Encircling One, She Who Melts, She Who Weeps, She Renowned as the Dancer, She of the Crest, She of the Topknot, She of Matted Locks, and in the vajra wheel the Unborn Yamī, She Who Shakes, She Who Terrifies, She Who Fulfills Desires, and the Great Blazing One); the grouping of words into names in these lists is also uncertain in places. The lines on the first continent and the Guide preceding the list of twelve Herukas are terse and their construal is uncertain. "Tamarisk wood" renders spen pa, which could also mean the planet Saturn. Many names in the lists of the thirty-six messengers, the sky-going musicians and the bird-faced yoginīs are not securely identifiable; where the Tibetan transliterates a Sanskrit form it is given as spelled, and where it translates one that cannot be identified it is rendered by meaning. Particularly uncertain are "the attendant" (kha ma), "the woman beaten by the king," the bird names "bya gar," "burning owl," "willow throat," "Hormo," and "Enemy of Deer," and the nāga "Balacara" (stobs kyi rgyu), possibly a rendering of another nāga name. The grounds named are Radiant (here probably Prabhākarī), Radiating Light for the sky wheel (likely Arciṣmatī), and Difficult to Purify (likely Sudurjayā). Many of the animal names in the lists of the earth and water wheels are uncertain; transliterated forms are kept as spelled, and several Tibetan names (Khasgomā, the wild one, She of the water cave) are rendered by meaning or by sound because they could not be identified. The list of kinswomen in the fire wheel is compressed and several of the kinship terms could be construed differently. The phrase rendered "the supplementary layer of the enjoyment body" ('phar ma) is uncertain. Most of the yoginīs in these lists bear names that are translated into Tibetan from Sanskrit forms that cannot be securely identified, so they are rendered by meaning; known names (Tilottamā, Padminī, Śaṅkhinī, Citriṇī, Hastinī, Gaurī and her companions, Vaitaraṇī, and the heavens of the three realms) are given in their Sanskrit or standard forms. The directional guardians of the charnel grounds here are the planets: Sun, Moon, Mars (Son of the Earth), Mercury (born of the Moon, the Hare-Marked), Jupiter (the Guru), Venus (Śukra), Saturn (Son of the Sun), and Rāhu (the Asura Head). Here གཏུམ་མོ, among the four corner goddesses with Pukkasī, Śavarī and Ḍombinī, is rendered Caṇḍālī as that set requires, though it was rendered Caṇḍī elsewhere. The lines on the ground "Possessed of the Wheel" in the wheel of mind are terse and uncertain. The names of the door guardians (the Water-Born, Vajra Dancer, the mare-faced one) and of three corner goddesses (Vajra Blazing Face, Vajra Frowning Face, Vajra Cleaver) are rendered from the Tibetan meanings, since their Sanskrit forms cannot be identified with confidence; "Gāndhārī" reflects the Tibetan transcription. In the list of heroes, the line read here as Viśvaḍāka and Padmaḍāka could also be a single name, and "Ratna" is taken as Ratnaḍāka; the last several heroes (the Steadfast-Minded, Liberation, Wisdom, Means, the Vajra of Mind) are translated by meaning. Among the sites, Mumuniya, "Conduct," the place of gatherings, the family field, the mountain's edge, the field of mantra, the city of the gods, the land of the Pervading Lord's deeds, the Yoginī, and the Dense City are rendered literally or as transcribed because their Sanskrit identifications are uncertain; the division of the last lines between village borders and near village borders follows the Tibetan word order. The mantra-extraction section is a coded letter grid: directions name the compartments of the diagram (Agni southeast, Nairṛti southwest, Īśāna northeast, the rākṣasa's quarter southwest), "segments" appear to denote vowel marks or diacritic elements, and "fire," "water," and "wind" denote the seed letters associated with those elements. The quarter called "the spirit's quarter" (Tibetan 'dre) and the sense of "pervading lord" in this section are uncertain and are rendered literally. "As was declared" may instead refer to a text called the Abhidhāna. This section continues a coded extraction of the root mantra from a lettered diagram. Directions name compartments of the grid, "fire," "water," "wind," and "the great power" denote element seed letters, and "segments" appear to be vowel signs or diacritics; the precise referents of "the spirit's quarter," "the wind's ground," "the all-going," and "the matrix" are uncertain and are rendered literally. This continues the coded extraction of the root mantra. Compartments of the diagram are named by direction, "segments" and "compartments" appear to designate vowel signs and other diacritics, and terms such as "the sun," "the son of fire," "the spirit's quarter," and "the wind's ground" are rendered literally because their exact referents in the letter grid are uncertain. This continues the coded extraction of the root mantra from the lettered diagram. Directional compartments, element seeds ("fire," "water," "wind," "the great power"), "segments," "the sun," "the empty one," and the expressions "beyond the ground" and "the ground's end" are rendered literally, since their exact referents in the grid are uncertain. This is a coded mantra-extraction (mantroddhāra) passage referring to positions in a letter grid arranged by the directions and the corners of the world protectors; the references (for example "the earth of Vāyu", "the spirit's quarter", "Agni's son") are rendered literally, since their exact letter values depend on the grid, which is not given here. "Compartment" renders both ལེའུ་ཚེ and རེའུ་མིག, and "segment" renders དུམ (བུ), as in the preceding piece. The line "This secret mantra is proclaimed as the accomplishment and compassion of Citriṇī" could also be read as "the compassion of manifold accomplishments." As in the preceding pieces, this is a coded mantra-extraction passage in which letters are identified by their positions in a directional grid; the positional references are rendered literally. "Forty less one" is the text's way of numbering the thirty-ninth foot. Indra's gate (དབང་པོའི་སྒོ) refers to the eastern position. In "the lord who accomplishes manifold aims," སྣ་ཚོགས is read as the adjective "manifold" rather than as the name Citriṇī. The phrase སར་རྟོགས་འབྱུང་པོ is unclear; it is taken here as "the bhūtas on the earth," reading ས as "earth," but it could also mean the bhūtas who come to know the place. The final sentence about the mantra's power reads literally "has not occurred and will not occur," meaning that nothing equal to it has been or will be. རྫོགས་ལྡན, གསུམ་ལྡན, གཉིས་ལྡན and རྩོད་དུས are the four world ages, rendered as the Kṛta age, the Tretā, the Dvāpara and the age of strife (Kali). The verses on them are terse and their exact sense is uncertain; they seem to teach that every age is present in every other. "The single quarter of the Tretā age" renders གསུམ་ལྡན་རྐང་པ་གཅིག, which is obscure. The goddess གཤིན་རྗེ་མ is rendered Yamī. The count of views adds up to seventy-four (five, two, seven, eight, sixteen and thirty-six); the line "less two, those of the seventh and the eighth" evidently reduces this to the stated seventy-two, though which items are meant is unclear. ཧེ་རུ་ཀ་མ is taken as a feminine form, "the Heruka goddess." In chapter nineteen, གར་དབང་ཕྱུག is rendered "the Lord of Dance" where it is explained as a term, and the deity's name as Padmanarteśvara in the colophon. The verses on the third and fourth chapters and the action and conduct tantras are terse and their reference is uncertain. Here དབང་པོ is "the senses," not the god Indra. "All-Terrifying One" (ཀུན་སྐྲག་མ) and "Fierce-Making One" (གཏུམ་བྱེད་མ) are rendered literally; I could not identify their Sanskrit names with confidence. "Two empowerments" (དབང་གཉིས) could also mean the two organs in union. "Sign" (མཚོན) and "sought" (འདྲི) are tentative readings of terse lines. ge ku da ha na are the code syllables for the five meats. The "six-stringed instrument" (དྲུག་ལྡན, "having six") is a guess. "Circle" (འཁོར་ལོ) in the feast section means the gathering of participants (gaṇacakra), not a drawn diagram. གཞོན་ནུ་མ here is an ordinary young maiden (kumārī), not the goddess Kaumārī. are is a vernacular Indic exclamation used as a refrain in the songs, and śambu seems to be a refrain or invocation; both are left as written. Here the "circle" (འཁོར་ལོ) in the feast chapter is the gathering of participants (gaṇacakra). "Liberate" (བསྒྲལ) in the song means to free beings, not the wrathful sense. The line on the cord's thickness ("twenty less one") is terse and its exact measure is unclear. "Abhidhāna" (མངོན་པར་བརྗོད་པ) is taken to refer to the explanatory tantra of that name; it could also simply mean "explicitly stated." The list of hearths in the body gives no triangular hearth; it is translated as it stands. "Hearth" among the sage's emblems (ཐབ) is odd as an attribute, but it is kept as written. "A measure of coin" renders the obscure རྡོ་ཞོ, apparently a weighed payment, and "seating in rows" renders the bare word གྲལ; both are tentative. The four goddesses of the ninefold maṇḍala (All-Beautiful Lady, Lady of Supreme Joy, Lady Most Playful, Vajra Lady of Delight) are translated literally; I could not securely identify their Sanskrit names. In the consecration chapter the text reads the opening words of a tantra, "Thus have I heard at one time, the Blessed One...", as syllables of purity: e, baṃ, ma, yā spell evaṃ mayā. "Wind" (རླུང) here is the breath, not the god Vāyu. "The four, laughing and the rest" refers to the four classes of tantra, characterized as laughing, gazing, holding hands and union. Sumbha (གནོད་མཛེས) is the wrathful king whose mantra is used to drive the dagger. Only the channel names that clearly match the standard list are given in Sanskrit. The Excellent One, the Empowering One, the Real One, the One of Joyful Speech, the Old Woman, the One Agreeable to All People, the Trembling One, the One of the Essence and the Goose are literal renderings, and Prabandromā is a partly transliterated name I could not identify. The list names fewer than thirty-six channels. This chapter gives the secret sign language (chommā) by which yogins and yoginīs recognize one another: a gesture is shown and the proper counter-sign is given, followed by its inner yogic meaning. Several body-part terms are tentative. The "nameless finger" is the ring finger; "pointing finger" renders སྟོན་པ, read as the forefinger. "Birth" (སྐྱེ་བ) as the answer to the throat is kept as written, though a body part may be meant. "Fierce fire" renders གཏུམ་མོ here as the inner heat (caṇḍālī), not the goddess Caṇḍī. Pracaṇḍā (རབ་གཏུམ) is taken as the first of the twenty-four goddesses of the Cakrasaṃvara maṇḍala. Makara is the zodiac sign (Capricorn). khara is the name of a year in the sixty-year cycle. The measures of the day, month and year are given in the Indian system of numerical code words (bhūtasaṃkhyā), in which sky and void stand for zero, eye and hand for two, taste for six, orifice for nine and mountain for seven; they are rendered here as the words themselves. The place names གང་བའི་རི and འབར་བ་འཛིན་པ are taken as the pīṭhas Pūrṇagiri and Jālandhara, in keeping with the closing reference to pīṭhas and upapīṭhas. The line "born from one's own mother" (རང་གི་མ་ལས་སྐྱེས) is obscure in this context and is rendered literally. The words in the list of signs are left in their Sanskrit or Apabhraṃśa form, as the text gives them as coded sign words; the list that follows gives their meanings in sequence. The item pa ḍu ba in that second list is likewise left as written. The syllable read as "fitting" (ཙྪམ་པ) is taken as ཚམ་པ, "suitable", which the context seems to require. "The dagger of Saturn" (སྤེན་པ་ཡི་ཕུར་བུ) may instead mean "Saturn and Jupiter" or a peg of a particular wood; the sense is uncertain. The "secret signs" (ཙྪོ་མ, chomā) are the coded gestures and words by which yoginīs and yogins recognize one another. The terms nu baṃ and "the twelve humans" are obscure in context and are rendered as written. "The White Vajra Lady" (རྡོ་རྗེ་དཀར་མོ) has not been identified with a Sanskrit name with confidence and is translated by meaning. The couplet "If one does not remain, one proceeds to gnosis; humans become rulers of men" appears to describe the door of the eyes, which the list implies but the line does not name; the sense is uncertain. The code-language glosses in the new chapter (thirty-third) are given as the Tibetan states them, even where the Sanskrit word ordinarily means something else; the line beginning "Birth: phalgu" is obscure and its division is uncertain. "The accomplishments that bear the names of mountains are as the twenty-eight remedies" is literal; the sense seems to be a link between the rites and the twenty-eight lunar mansions that follow. In the list of planets, "Mars" reads the evident "son of the earth" (Bhauma) for the written "mother of the earth." "Drumacchāyā" renders the Tibetan "tree shade," the goddess whose mantra closes the passage. "The Middle Way" may also refer here to the central channel. The opening of the thirty-fourth chapter reads literally "Then by the Blessed Lord," followed by a request ("please teach"); it is rendered as a request addressed to him. "Earth Guardian" translates a goddess's name that could not be identified with confidence in Sanskrit; the mantra that closes her chapter is addressed to Airāvatī. The lines "In the field are forms such as kalā" and "the limit of the touch of wood" are literal renderings of obscure phrases. "Central channel" renders the term that elsewhere means the Middle Way; here it plainly names the channel into which the drop descends. The goddesses "the Kisser," "She Who Transforms," and "She Who Follows" are translated from the Tibetan, as their Sanskrit names could not be identified with confidence. Oḍi is given as the Tibetan spells it; it may stand for Oḍra or Oḍḍiyāna. "Central channel" again renders the term elsewhere meaning the Middle Way, as the context is the channels. The "gargaṭa" whose dung is used for incense is given as the Tibetan spells it and could not be identified with confidence; "cchomaka" and "cchomā" are the secret signs of the yoginīs. The goddess Śyāmadevī ("the Green Goddess") is identified from her mantra. In this sexual context "liṅga" is the male organ, not the effigy of a wrathful rite. The "piśāca plant," the "earth creeper," "horse scent" and the "sole-hero plant" are literal renderings of plant names that could not be identified with confidence; "the remainder of the rite of great love" is also literal. The syllables placed in the forty-nine squares spell out the mantra of Sumbharāja; Vairocana stands for oṃ, and the text gives the fortieth square twice, as it stands. "The unchanging substance" renders rnam 'gyur med pa literally; in this ritual context it is probably a coded term for a bodily substance. "Become crippled" reads phye ba as phye bo, "lame," as the release by water in the next lines requires an immobilizing rite. The eight nāga kings are correlated with the ten winds (prāṇa, apāna, samāna, vyāna, and the secondary winds nāga, kūrma, kṛkara, devadatta, dhanañjaya); "meditative concentration" (bsam gtan) stands where udāna would be expected, and is kept as written. "The nāga Jaya" and "Vijaya" render rgyal ba and rnam par rgyal ba as nāga names. "The Later Saṃvara" (bde mchog phyi ma) is taken as a reference to the Saṃvara explanatory or later tantra. "The first Veda" in the yantra list is kept literally; it presumably codes a syllable. Aśvakarṇā is taken as the Sanskrit name behind rta rna ma, "the Horse-Eared Goddess." In the line about the tenth position (bcu par ni yig de bzhin), ni is read as a particle, giving "likewise, that syllable"; it could also be read as the syllable ni. The closing words bdag 'dri, "I ask," are taken to open the question continued in the next piece. Khagānanā, "the Bird-Faced One," is taken as the Sanskrit name behind bya gdong ma, supported by the vocative khagānane in the mantra. In the fifteenth position of the preta yantra, the Tibetan names no syllable other than ni, so ni is taken as the syllable itself. "Piśāca root" renders sha za'i rtsa ba, apparently a plant so called. Cakravegā renders 'khor shugs ma, confirmed by cakra bege in the mantra; Khaṇḍarohā renders dum skyes ma. "Three with a half" renders phyed bcas gsum literally; it may mean two and a half. "The syllable of wind" in the eighth position is kept as the text names it, without supplying the syllable. "The perch of a vulture" renders bya rgod tshad stan, whose second element is unclear. "Beautiful in harming" renders gnod mdzes literally; it may be an epithet whose identity is uncertain. "The target, in five sections" renders le'u tshe lnga ru bsgrub bya, also unclear. In "draw the yantra," bre bya is read as bri bya, "should be drawn." Śauṇḍinī, "the Wine-Seller," is supported by śauṇḍinīye in the mantra. The "mantra of a hundred feet" renders rkang pa brgya pa literally; it may instead mean a mantra of a hundred lines or syllable units, and its identity is uncertain. The line rendered "so it enters among humans" (mi'i 'jug pa) is obscure. Yid bzang ma is taken as the name Sumanā; it may simply mean "the good-minded woman." The "fine fiber" renders bad snal, a word of uncertain sense. In the closing list of forty-nine segments, the Tibetan as it stands passes from the twenty-fourth to the twenty-sixth, giving no syllable for the twenty-fifth; the translation follows the text. The new chapter correlates the site Suvarṇadvīpa with mindfulness as a limb of awakening, following this tantra's pattern of matching sacred sites with the factors of awakening. The "forty-eight feet" (rkang pa bzhi bcu rtsa brgyad pa) probably refers to the lines or units of the root mantra; the exact sense is uncertain. The line "dissolving into one's own wheel-turning monarch" (rang gi 'khor los sgyur thim pa) is obscure. "The end of the twelve" renders bcu gnyis mtha', the yogic term dvādaśānta. In the line on the gaze, don yod mthong ba is taken as "an unfailing gaze" rather than as a name. The goddess's name Suvīrā is confirmed by her mantra; gnod mdzes is taken as Sumbha, as in the title Sumbharāja. The line "grinds to dust upon the ground of the great power" (dbang chen mthil la phye mar byed) is obscure; dbang chen may refer to the earth maṇḍala (Mahendra). "The night of the fourteenth, the pretas' night" renders yi dags bcu bzhi, apparently the fourteenth of the dark fortnight. The list said to be the eight accomplishments names nine items in the Tibetan, given here as they stand. The lines about the "youthful one" and being "seized by the night" are obscure yogic descriptions of the movement of the drop through the channels, translated literally. "The sum of space, time and fire" (mkha' dus me) appears to use the Indian convention of number words (space for zero, time for six, fire for three), read in reverse as 360, the traditional number of the body's joints. "The pendant" renders 'phyang ba, apparently a yogic term for something hanging within the body (perhaps the uvula, Skt. lambikā); its exact reference is uncertain. Gshin dgra is taken as Yamāri. The lines on support and supported, and on wisdom and means, are translated literally; their argument is compressed. The verses from Kapālin to Vajrasattva play on the names of the twenty-four heroes of the Cakrasaṃvara maṇḍala, which are given here in their Sanskrit forms. "The numbers of the natural elements are the hours, the nāgas and the arrows" seems to use number words (nāgas for eight, arrows for five), but the number intended is uncertain. In the list of bodily constituents, several pairs are obscure; "the absence of fever" renders rims med as it stands. "The Later Abhidhāna" is taken as the Abhidhānottara Tantra; "the Arising of All Conduct" (kun spyod 'byung ba) may refer to the Saṃvarodaya, but this is uncertain. "Touchstone" renders spar sha (Skt. sparśa). The sixteen syllables in the code verses (ra, hā, sya, pa, ra, me, ra, me, sa, rbā, tma, na, sa, dā, sthi, ta) spell out the Sanskrit of the preceding line "When one delights in the supreme secret, the self of all always dwells" (rahasye parame ramye sarvātmani sadā sthitaḥ), each assigned a substance. Several substance names are uncertain: "mountain-born" probably means mineral pitch (śilājatu), "hollow-born" is literal, rābiṇa is left unidentified, and "flesh" reads the bare syllable ཤ as the Tibetan word for flesh. The commentary on the twelfth verse line reads "drink of peace" (ཞི་བ) where the root verse has "drink of the moon" (ཟླ་བ); I have translated each as written. ཁ་ཆེ is taken as Kashmiri saffron and གྲོ་ག as birch bark. བོན in "invoked by all the deities" is unclear. The passage correlates the twelve links of dependent origination with the twelve zodiacal signs and months, partly under transliterated, nonstandard Sanskrit names (rohita, mohita, briṣa, raṇḍaka, mikira, bidri, brika, bhidrika, bhitri), which are left as written; "the Auspicious One" renders བཟང་པོ, and "the Widow" (ཡུགས་ས་མོ) seems to correspond to raṇḍa. In "born on the day of rohita" I take ཉ་མ as a slip for ཉི་མ, "day." "Those who are led" (འདྲེན་མ) is uncertain. The numerical verses give the lengths of day and night in periods (nāḍikās) of a sixty-period day, through the northern and southern courses of the sun and the equinox (viṣu). "Sky, hollow, eye" and "sky, sky, taste, moon, eye" are coded numerals (sky 0, hollow 9, eye 2, taste 6, moon 1); the latter read in reverse gives 21,600, the daily count of breaths stated earlier. "The dusty ones" (རྡུལ་ཅན) stands where night would be expected and may reflect Sanskrit rajanī, "night," read as rajas, "dust." "By the time of the months" for ནམ་ཟླ is uncertain. The vowel series repeats the syllable i where e would be expected; it is translated as written. The "transferences" (འཕོ་བ) appear to be shifts of the breath or vital wind correlated with the links of dependent origination as the days of illness and death. Many of the yoginīs and kṣetrapālas (field protectors) here are named by Tibetan renderings whose Sanskrit originals I could not identify with confidence; these are translated by meaning (for example Great Face, Twisted Ear, Fearsome Eyes, Great Shanks). Several sacred places and trees appear in irregular transliteration and are given as written, apart from established names such as Jālandhara (འབར་བ་འཛིན), Kashmir, Rājagṛha and Tibet. The lines on the birthplaces ("the body of the scattered," ཆོལ་པའི་ལུས) are obscure. Most goddess and kṣetrapāla names in this list are rendered by meaning, as in the preceding pieces, because no secure Sanskrit form could be identified. "Vipanasamā," "Siddhasiṃpaḍi," "Eranya," "Kampo" and "Jamme" are transcribed from the Tibetan letters and are uncertain; the line "At Vāruṇya, at Siddhasiṃpaḍi" gives no seat in the Tibetan. "Puṇḍravardhana" is the evident place behind the Tibetan spelling Puṇḍavadha. "Upon coarse cloth" takes རེ as རེ་བ, which is uncertain. The line "And the wise, become as nothing at all" is obscure in the Tibetan. "The day of the bhūtas" is taken as the fourteenth lunar day (bhūtacaturdaśī). "Pīṭhas and upapīṭhas" renders the paired sacred-site terms གནས and ཉེ་གནས. "Possession" for བརླབ་གདོལ is tentative. The lone syllable ན in "If at that juncture" is read as the conditional particle; the line is obscure. "Bared Fangs" translates མཆེ་བ་རྣམ་གཙིགས, the name of the samādhi and deity, by meaning. "Bringing under control" reads དབང་དུ་འོད as དབང་དུ་འོང, an evident slip; "placed" likewise reads ད་གོད as དགོད. The titles in the list of tantras (Samāja, Saṃvara, Uttara, Udaya, Tattvasaṃgraha) are identified from their standard Tibetan short forms; "the Abhidhāna of three hundred thousand" refers to the root Saṃvara explanatory tantra said to be the source of the Laghusaṃvara, and "Khasama" to the tantra "Equal to Space." In the list of gods, སྐྱོབ་པ ("the Protector") and the following བླ ("the Lofty One") could not be identified and are translated literally. ཁྱིམ་ཐབ is taken in its usual sense of "consort, spouse." The dialogue after the fourth chapter's colophon is uncertain in its division: the goddess's request is read with གསུངས as an honorific request, and "the enemy of the gods" (ལྷ་དགྲ) is taken as opening the Lord's reply, though its sense here is obscure. The zodiac and timing verses are terse; "the Deer," "the Hare" and "Skanda" are translated literally, since their correspondence to standard sign names is unclear, as is "the five solar ones" (ཉི་ཅན་ལྔ). སྦྱིན་བྱེད, rendered "the bestowers," names a class paired with the gods and linked to the planets and signs; its Sanskrit equivalent is uncertain. The eight-spoke wheel verse assigning abbreviated sign syllables to the quarters is obscure; "the Childless One" (བུ་མེད) is translated literally and its function is unclear. The lunar mansions and planets are identified from their standard Tibetan astrological names; "the Head" (མགོ) beside Mṛgaśirṣa, the meaning of དོ་ལ ("dola"), and "wealth in hand is lost" (ལག་ནོར་མེད) are uncertain. "Vajra Light" translates rdo rje 'od literally; it may stand for a deity name such as Vajraprabha, parallel to Amitābha in the preceding chapter, but this cannot be confirmed. Rohita is a division (muhūrta) of the day. Several counts use the Indian system of number words: "the seasons" stands for six, "the Vedas" for four, and "the suns" for twelve; "twice six," "twice seven," and "twice eight" stand for twelve, fourteen, and sixteen. The text reads "fifty days" (lnga bcu) where the sequence from fourteen to sixteen requires fifteen; this is translated as the evident reading bco lnga. "The rider on scent" is a standard epithet of the wind. "The Upward-Gazing One" and "the Upward-Facing One" render channel names whose Sanskrit forms cannot be identified with confidence. Several lines in this passage, such as "the one that is declared is adorned," are terse and their exact sense is uncertain. Here ནག་པ in "the eighth and fourteenth" is taken as the dark fortnight (nag pa'i phyogs), the usual days for such rites, rather than the lunar mansion Citrā. "She of the Fire Seat" (me gdan mo), "the one with a human seat" (mi stan can), and "the Lamp" or "Lamp Lady" (mar me, mar me ma) are literal renderings of names I cannot identify with confidence; mar me ma may stand for a goddess elsewhere called Lāmā, and mi stan can may be an epithet of Kubera or another guardian. "The Playful One" (rol ldan ma) and "the Black-Faced One" (gdong nag mo) are likewise literal renderings of channel names. "Twelve threads" may refer to the measure of the breath's outward extent in finger-breadths. The phrase rendered "one is in doubt" (the tshom za) appears twice where the pattern elsewhere has "without doubt"; it is translated as written. The final list consists of syllable codes, apparently abbreviations combining lunar mansions, weekdays (for example bu for Budha, gu for Guru, śu for Śukra, ma for Maṅgala), and lunar dates, followed by their omens; they are kept as written. In mo bu mo the syllables bu mo could also be read as the Tibetan for Virgo, but the pattern of three-syllable codes suggests they belong to the code. "The messenger who travels on six feet" may refer to a bee, an image for the moving breath. "The sprouting one" translates myu gu can, a term whose Sanskrit source I cannot identify with confidence. "Triple banner" and "with dust" (rdul can) are literal renderings of uncertain attributes. The opening lines assign named days or periods to the eight directions, apparently: the southeast (seat of Agni), the south (Yama), the southwest (the rākṣasa), the west (Varuṇa), the northwest (Prabhañjana, the wind), the north (the Treasure, Kubera's direction), the northeast (the Three-Eyed One), and the east. Mikira and Raṇḍā are given as the Tibetan spells them; "the Widow" (yugs sa mo) is evidently the Tibetan for the same name Raṇḍā. Distraction, the Dreaming One, and the fang are literal renderings of period names I cannot identify with confidence. Vajrajaṭila ("Vajra with Matted Locks") is reconstructed from rdo rje ral pa can. "The Vajra Cause" translates rdo rje rgyu as an epithet of the speaker. The numbers in the last section count the lunar mansions beginning with Aśvinī. "The three eastern ones" and "the three northern ones" evidently correspond to the three Pūrva and the three Uttara mansions. "The manifest juncture" renders mngon par tshigs mtshams, apparently the linking of rebirth. "The city of the senses" (dbang po'i grong khyer) could also be read as "the city of Indra." "The Exceedingly Excellent One" (shin tu bzang po) and Vajrabhadra name the presiding forms of these chapters; the Sanskrit of the first (perhaps Atibhadra or Subhadra) cannot be confirmed. The sense of "comes to be experienced" (nyams par 'gyur) is uncertain. "The element of touch" renders the transliterated Sanskrit sparśa. The opening list assigns the lunar mansions to animal classes (yoni); it is rendered as the text gives it, including the apparent overlap of "the first" with both the donkey and the horse. Here ཁྱུ་མཆོག is the animal "bull," whereas in the list of signs below it is the zodiac sign Taurus. Aries, Libra, Cancer and Makara correspond to the movable signs, the second group to the fixed, and the third to the dual signs; "lofty" and "the ones that lead" are literal renderings. ངན་སྤོང ("Abandoner of Evil") and བཀྲ་ཤིས ("the Auspicious One") are planetary epithets I cannot identify with confidence (by Indian convention the female and neuter planets would include Venus and Saturn), so they are translated literally. དབུ་མ / དབུས་མ is taken here as the central channel (avadhūtī), not the Madhyamaka school. མིག་མི་བཟང (Virūpākṣa) here names the tantric deity presiding over the sixteenth chapter, not the guardian king of the west. དབང་པོ here means the sense faculties, not Indra; རླུང is the wind, not the god Vāyu; མེ is fire, not Agni; and ཆུའི་སྲང is a unit of water-clock time (pala), not Libra. ཆུ་ཚོད ("water-hour") renders the nāḍikā. དབུ་མ in "the messenger of the central channel" is taken as the avadhūtī. The opening line of the Goddess's request uses an instrumental (བདག་པོ་ཆེན་པོས), read as "May the Great Lord teach"; the following lines are taken as the Lord's reply. "The letter ha joined with the sixth" is read as a seed syllable formed from ha and the sixth vowel (presumably hūṃ), but the text gives only this description. In "subjugation and the rest," དབང is the subjugating rite, not empowerment. ཨཱ་ག in the list of practices not to be performed is unclear; it is kept as "āga," possibly a short form of a rite such as invocation (āgamana). Here དབུ་མ / དབུས་མ is taken as the central channel. སྤང་བ in the rites with substances is taken as averting or expelling. Here དྲག་པོ means "fierce" (darkness) and is not the name Rudra; མེ is fire, not Agni. དོ་ལཱ་ཧཱ་ར ("dolāhāra") is kept as written; it seems to name a yogic technique, perhaps of "swinging" the breath, but cannot be identified with confidence. In "Saṃvara in bodily form" (གཟུགས་སུ་སྡོམ་པ), སྡོམ་པ is taken as the deity Saṃvara rather than "vow." Here དབང་པོ is read as "the faculties," not Indra, and རླུང as wind, not Vāyu. རྡུལ is taken in its tantric sense of the female generative fluid (rajas). The chapter's closing epithet ("at the limit of the fourth") is obscure in the Tibetan and is translated literally. The six letters given for Padmanarteśvara spell out his name syllable by syllable. The letter ta in the line on śva is uncertain in sense. "Words of the junctures" (མཚམས་ཀྱི་སྐད) seems to mean a coded or intentional language; བདེ་མཆོག་ཕྱི་མ is read as the title of the "Later Saṃvara" (an uttaratantra of the Saṃvara cycle). Here དབང་གི་ལས means the rite of subjugation, not empowerment; དབང་པོ is "the faculties," not Indra. ཟླ་བ་རྡོ་རྗེ ("the moon vajra") may be a deity's name (Candravajra), but is translated by meaning. The sites in the final list are the standard pīṭhas and related places, given in their Indian forms. The numbers in the breath passage are consistent: 576 transitions of 37.5 breaths each give the 21,600 breaths of a day and night stated in the next lines. The "three mothers" (ཕྱི་མོ་གསུམ) are probably letter-sets (mātṛkā) alongside the vowels; rendered literally. "Twenty-five principles" in the chapter title versus "twenty-four" in the verses follows the Tibetan as it stands. "The water-born" (ཆུ་སྐྱེས) is a standard word for the lotus, but it can also mean the conch; which object the yoginīs stir is unclear, so it is kept literal. The verses telling one to take what is not given, kill, lie and go to "the mother" are the antinomian injunctions of yoginī tantra, which the tradition reads in a coded, non-literal sense; they are translated as written. The Indian paṇḍita རྒྱལ་བའི་སྡེ is given as Jayasena, the known collaborator of the translator Dharma Yönten; Yutung Lhündrup, the temple's name, is given in phonetics. The reviser "possessing the eye of Dharma" may be a description or a veiled name; it is translated as a description.
